IV
 EXAMPLES OF CASES EXAMINED IN 2024
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
CHAPTER IV
EXAMPLES OF CASES 
EXAMINED IN 2025
Case No. 1: Processing of personal data concerning health without implementing sufficient 
measures to ensure their confidentiality and security.
The NCPDP initiated ex officio proceedings under Article 20(1)(a), (i) and Article 27(4) of Law 
No. 133/2011 on personal data protection, regarding alleged vulnerabilities on the website of a 
medical analysis laboratory, related to a breach of security of special categories of personal data.
During the examination of the accumulated material, it was established that any person, accessing 
the “Company organizational chart” option and modifying the last 3 digits of the link, could view 
or even download files containing personal data of the data subjects, namely: name, surname, 
date, month, year of birth, home address (in some cases) and medical information concerning their 
health status.
Thus, based on the materials gathered during the investigation initiated on the basis of the ex 
officio request, it was confirmed that the medical analysis laboratory had not implemented the 
necessary organizational and technical measures to protect personal data against destruction, 
modification, blocking, copying, dissemination, or other unlawful actions, measures intended to 
ensure an appropriate level of security in view of the risks presented by the processing and the 
nature of the processed data.
In this context, it was found that the medical analysis laboratory processed the personal data of 
the data subjects/clients in breach of the requirements provided by Article 4(1)(a), Article 29(1) and 
Article 30(1) of the Law on personal data protection.
In the exercise of its statutory responsibilities, the NCPDP 
continuously monitors the manner in which data controllers 
comply with the requirements of personal data protection 
legislation. In this context, the Authority identifies and analyzes 
recurring issues, deficiencies and non-compliant practices 
arising in the processing of personal data.
Through its annual activity report, the NCPDP seeks to provide 
a comprehensive overview of the main categories of cases 
examined, the issues addressed during supervisory procedures 
and the remedial measures applied, thereby ensuring the 
protection of data subjects’ rights in relation to the processing of their personal data. The 
presentation of relevant cases serves to highlight both the risks associated with specific 
processing activities and the preventive measures necessary to avoid similar breaches.
Accordingly, the cases examined in 2025 reflect the sectors where the most frequent non-
compliances were identified, the types of infringements detected and the approach adopted by 
the Authority in analyzing and resolving them.

=== PAGE 92 ===
www.datepersonale.md
92
IV
 EXAMPLES OF CASES EXAMINED IN 2024
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
Case No. 2: Collection of personal data by unauthorized persons.
The NCPDP examined a notification from the Central Electoral Commission (CEC), regarding the 
collection of signatures by a person in support of a candidate for the position of President of the 
Republic of Moldova.
During the investigation, it was determined that the initiative group, consisting of 100 persons, for 
collecting signatures in support of a candidate nominated by a political party, was registered with 
the CEC; however, the person in question was not part of the list of members of the initiative group 
who had the right to collect signatures of the candidate’s supporters. The following personal data 
were collected in the signature lists: name, surname, year of birth, signature, series and number of 
the identity card.
Considering the presented circumstances, the NCPDP noted that the process of collecting personal 
data, as a processing operation, must be carried out correctly, based on an adequate legal 
framework, in compliance with applicable norms in the field of data protection and after obtaining 
all authorizations required by law, the absence of which poses a risk of violating the fundamental 
rights of the data subjects.
Thus, by collecting signatures without being part of a legally constituted initiative group 
and without obtaining prior authorization from the CEC, the person in question breached 
the provisions of Article 4(1)(a) of the Law on personal data protection, which regulates the 
fundamental principles/basic conditions for processing personal data.
Case No. 3: Disclosure of personal data that does not constitute information of public interest.
The NCPDP examined a complaint from a data subject who alleged non-compliant processing 
operations of special categories of personal data concerning him, manifested by the transmission 
by a Police Inspectorate to a Local Public Authority (LPA) of information stored in the materials of 
a contravention case, initiated based on a complaint by the data subject’s mother, as well as the 
failure by the person in a position of responsibility within the LPA to grant the data subject’s right 
of access to his personal data.
During the investigation, the Police Inspectorate invoked that the disclosure of the data/information 
to the person in a position of responsibility within the LPA was carried out based on the data 
subject’s function as a public official and Law No. 148/2023 on access to information of public 
interest.
Applying the provisions of Article 6(2) of Law No. 148/2023 on access to information of public 
interest and Article 8(1)(f) of the aforementioned law, it was determined that access to information 
of public interest may be limited in accordance with the proportionality criterion provided in 
Article 9, if the disclosure of the information would prejudice the protection of personal data. At 
the same time, Article 8(3) of the same law stipulates that, by way of derogation from paragraph 
(1)(f), it is prohibited to restrict access to information of public interest that constitutes personal 
data concerning the professional activity of public officials, within the meaning of the Integrity 
Law No. 82/2017, including: name and surname; position; education; professional experience; 
remuneration; conflicts of interest; non-expunged disciplinary sanctions.

=== PAGE 93 ===
www.datepersonale.md
93
IV
 EXAMPLES OF CASES EXAMINED IN 2024
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
Case No. 4: Publication of personal data on social networks.
The NCPDP examined a complaint from data subjects who alleged a presumed non-compliant 
processing of personal data, manifested by the placement of photo/video images from which their 
home could be identified, accompanied by defamatory text, on an account on the  www.tiktok.
com social network.
During the investigation, it was determined that the complainant and the reported persons were 
relatives. Against the background of a conflictual relationship, a video material was published on 
the user’s account on the www.tiktok.com social network, disclosing the following personal data: 
the person’s name and surname, previously held positions (text inserted on the video); location 
and identification data of the home, showing in the video recording the address/location of the 
home, including images from inside the yard, voicing the intention to disclose the personal data 
to as many people as possible.
Additionally, it must be noted that the deliberate publication on social platforms of information 
or images containing personal data amounts to the person in question assuming the public 
nature of this data, in the sense that it becomes accessible to an undetermined number of 
people and susceptible to viewing, collection, processing, use, or disclosure by third parties. Under 
these conditions, personal data thus made public may be processed by third parties for various 
purposes, including illegal or abusive purposes and is likely to generate significant risks, as well as 
psychological, physical, or moral harm for the data subject.
Following the examination of the case, no legitimate necessity for disclosing the complainants’ 
personal data on the personal “TikTok” page was noted, nor was a legal basis identified that would 
justify the disclosure of the complainants’ personal data by publishing it on the “TikTok” social 
network.
In the examined case, the NCPDP found that the Police Inspectorate’s argument was not legally 
supported/legally argued, as the materials accumulated in the contravention case were not related 
to the data subject’s official activity and the representative authority of the population of the 
administrative-territorial unit and the executive body of the local council was not a party to the 
contravention case.
Furthermore, it was found that the processing of personal data, including special categories of data 
belonging to the data subject, carried out by transmitting the materials related to the contravention 
case from the Police Inspectorate to the LPA, could not be considered compliant. The disclosure of 
this information to third parties constitutes an interference in the private life of the data subject 
and his family members and a breach of the provisions of Article 4(1)(a) and (b), Article 6(1) and 
Article 7(1) of the Law on personal data protection was noted in this case.
Moreover, the NCPDP found the fact of the LPA’s failure to resolve the data subject’s request 
regarding the exercise of his right of access to his personal data, constituting a breach of Article 
13(1)(a) of the Law on  personal data protection.

=== PAGE 94 ===
www.datepersonale.md
94
IV
 EXAMPLES OF CASES EXAMINED IN 2024
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
Furthermore, the NCPDP points out that the circumstances of a conflict between relatives do not 
represent an event of heightened public interest and the negative impact on the complainants, as 
a result of the posting of the video recording, infringed upon the latter’s right to private life.
In this context, considering the categories of published personal data, the purpose of their 
publication in the internet space, the NCPDP determined that the processing of personal data 
in the case under examination could not be qualified as compliant, as the knowledge of these 
categories of data by an unlimited number of people represents an interference in the private life 
of the data subjects and a breach of the provisions of Article 4(1)(a), (b), (c) and Article 5(1) of the 
Law on personal data protection was noted in this case.
Case No. 5: Publication of identifying personal data without a legal basis.
Following a notification, the NCPDP conducted a verification of the information published on the 
webpage of the Official Information Storage Mechanism, in particular, the personal data recorded 
in the semi-annual reports of banking institutions licensed by the National Bank of Moldova.
Following the examination, reasonable suspicions were found regarding non-compliant processing 
of personal data by two banking institutions, through the disclosure/dissemination of the state 
identification numbers (IDNP) of certain natural persons affiliated with persons in positions of 
responsibility.
Under Articles 19-20 and Article 27(4) of Law No. 133/2011 on personal data protection, the 
NCPDP initiated ex officio proceedings to verify the compliance of the personal data processing by 
the two aforementioned banking institutions with applicable legal requirements in the field.
Thus, one of the banking institutions made public, both on its official website and through the 
Official Information Storage Mechanism, the semi-annual report, within which 47 IDNPs belonging 
to natural persons affiliated with persons in positions of responsibility were disclosed, without 
applying depersonalization or anonymization measures for this data. According to the Law on 
personal data protection, this action represents excessive and non-compliant data processing, 
carried out in breach of the principles of lawfulness, proportionality and confidentiality.
The other banking institution published on the official website of the Information Storage 
Mechanism the semi-annual report, which contained the IDNPs of 11 affiliated natural persons. 
This publication was carried out in breach of the provisions of Law No. 133/2011 on personal data 
protection, as this data was to be transmitted exclusively to competent authorities and was not 
intended for public disclosure.
Although the banking institution invoked legal transparency obligations, the NCPDP found the 
absence of an express legal basis justifying the publication of IDNPs, considering the processing to 
be excessive and disproportionate to the declared purpose. Consequently, the controller was held 
responsible for breaching the principles of lawfulness, confidentiality and proportionality in the 
processing of personal data.

=== PAGE 95 ===
www.datepersonale.md
95
IV
 EXAMPLES OF CASES EXAMINED IN 2024
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
Case No. 6: Collection of personal data and registration 
of persons on certain platforms for the purpose of obtaining monetary sums.
The NCPDP was notified by a police inspectorate regarding the alleged unauthorized collection 
of personal data belonging to two data subjects by certain natural persons, for the purpose of 
registering them on platforms made available by a political formation, with the aim of obtaining 
sums of money.
Following the examination of the accumulated materials and statements of the involved persons, 
the persons who carried out the operations of collecting the personal data contained in the identity 
documents, as well as the contact details of the data subjects, for the purpose of registering them 
on the respective party platforms, were identified.
From the case materials, it was noted that one of the persons involved in organizing the data 
collection exercised the function of local leader of a political party in the data subjects’ locality of 
residence. This person had duties of recruiting party activists and members, as well as coordinating 
data collection activities. The data thus collected was used for registering persons on so-called 
“bots,” with the aim of requesting financial aid originating from banking institutions in the Russian 
Federation.
Furthermore, the person in question did not present any legal act or probative document 
attesting to the existence of official instructions, mandates, or authorizations conferred by the 
political formation regarding the collection and registration of personal data under the described 
circumstances.
It is also relevant to mention that, following the steps taken by the NCPDP, the political formation 
did not present an official position or any justificatory documents confirming or refuting the 
statements of its representative.
At the same time, it could not be established that the processing operations of the complainants’ 
personal data, materialized by registering them on “bots” and on the platforms used for opening 
accounts intended for fund transfers from entities in the Russian Federation, were carried out on 
the basis of valid consent expressed by the data subjects.
In conclusion, a breach of the provisions of Article 4(1)(a) and (b), Article 5(1) and Article 32(5) 
of the Law on personal data protection was found in the processing operations of personal data 
carried out by the identified persons.
Although the banking institutions invoked obligations incumbent upon them under financial-
banking legislation, the NCPDP found the absence of a legal basis justifying the publication 
of IDNPs, as these were to be communicated exclusively to competent authorities, not to the 
general public. In this case, the NCPDP found a breach of Article 4(1), Article 9 and Article 29(1) 
of the Law on personal data protection, initiating contravention proceedings in accordance 
with Article 74/1(1) of the Contravention Code.
It is noted that the banking institutions demonstrated responsiveness by ensuring the 
depersonalization of the published data following the intervention of the NCPDP.

=== PAGE 96 ===
www.datepersonale.md
96
IV
 EXAMPLES OF CASES EXAMINED IN 2024
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
Case No. 7: Cross-border transfer of personal data 
to financial-banking institutions in another state.
The NCPDP examined complaints from several complainants residing in the ATU Gagauzia who 
reported receiving several messages mentioning the allocation of financial resources in their 
names by banks in the Russian Federation.
During the investigation, all complainants stated that they had not participated in meetings 
organized during 2023-2024 by political parties and had not transmitted their personal data 
to anyone; however, social aid received from the state was provided through the Post Office or 
by another secure method. Thus, the complainants assumed that members of a political party 
had made, on their behalf, registrations on the platforms used by banking entities in the Russian 
Federation, using information obtained from the Health and Social Assistance Directorate of ATU 
Gagauzia.
During the investigation, it was determined that, in the context of the operations complained of 
by the petitioners, the “Centrify,” “Qsms,” and “Authentify” applications/platforms are products of 
companies not headquartered in the territory of the Republic of Moldova. Furthermore, from the 
information presented in the public space, including by competent law enforcement bodies, it was 
noted that personal data had been collected and transferred cross-border to entities in the Russian 
Federation, as specifically entities from this country could make transfers in Russian rubles.
Based on the described circumstances, from the materials accumulated on this case, it was not 
possible to concretely determine the person/persons/entities that transmitted the personal data 
cross-border in the situations complained of by the petitioners.
Nevertheless, it is certain that processing of personal data manifested by the cross-border transfer 
thereof to a state that does not ensure an adequate level of protection took place, for purposes that 
may prejudice the fundamental rights and freedoms of the natural person regarding the personal 
data protection.
Thus, in rem, the NCPDP found a breach of the provisions of Article 32(5) of Law No. 133/2011 
on personal data protection, regarding the processing through cross-border transfer of the 
complainants’ personal data.
Case No. 8: Accessing personal data from the Real Estate Register.
A complaint from a natural person was received by the NCPDP, requesting verification of the 
lawfulness of the processing operations of his personal data carried out by a company through the 
Real Estate Register.
Under these circumstances, the NCPDP initiated verification of compliance with the provisions 
of Article 4(1)(a) and (b) and Article 13 of the Law on personal data protection, regarding the 
processing of the complainant’s personal data.
During the examination of the accumulated material, it was found that the company consulted 
information referring to 3 real estate properties containing personal data about the property 
owner; the registration basis - the name and data of the act by which the property right was 
acquired.

=== PAGE 97 ===
www.datepersonale.md
97
IV
 EXAMPLES OF CASES EXAMINED IN 2024
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
Case No. 9: Processing of personal data concerning health status.
Following the receipt of a request submitted by a district councillor, the NCPDP initiated ex officio 
proceedings and proceeded to examine the case within the limits of its legal powers, investigating 
an alleged fact of illegal processing of special categories of personal data of certain data subjects, 
caused by inappropriate actions imputable to the District President.
Following the gathering of evidence, it was established that the District President issued and 
signed a response addressed to district councillors regarding the behaviour of medical personnel 
in some medical points in the district.
To the response, the District President attached extracts from the medical records of some patients, 
containing: name, surname, date of birth, IDNP, home address and information concerning the 
persons’ (patients’) health status.
During the initiated investigation, the District President presented two consent declarations signed 
by some patients, invoking the existence of a legal basis for processing personal data, within the 
meaning of the provisions of the legislation on personal data protection.
In this case, it was noted that consent was obtained only from two data subjects, while the attached 
documents contained data of several persons. Furthermore, the formulations in the consents were 
general and unclear, not fulfilling the conditions for valid consent (freely given, specific, informed 
and unambiguous).
Moreover, the NCPDP noted that the transmission of medical records to councillors was neither 
necessary nor proportionate to the pursued aim, as verifying and subsequently justifying the work 
schedule of medical personnel in the response addressed to councillors could have been achieved 
through administrative means non-intrusive to the private life of the persons concerned.
In this case, it was noted that the processing of the data was to be conditioned by the consent 
of the data subject and, respectively, by his right to decide freely and unequivocally whether and 
when his personal data could be processed by the company, in compliance with the provisions of 
the Law on personal data protection.
Furthermore, the NCPDP did not consider as founded the argument invoked by the data controller 
that it had consulted the public part of the Real Estate Register, as the public part of the Register 
does not provide information about the identity of the owner, the property right over the real 
estate, or the mode of acquisition.
Thus, during the investigations, the NCPDP found that the company processed the data 
subject’s personal data without respecting the requirements provided by Article 4(1)(a) and 
(b) of Law No.133/2011.
At the same time, the company did not resolve the data subject’s request regarding the 
exercise of the right of access to personal data, not providing him with a reasoned/detailed 
response making available to him information about the processing operations of personal 
data carried out by the company and a breach of Article 13 of Law 133/2011 was also found. 
In this case, the NCPDP initiated contravention proceedings.

=== PAGE 98 ===
www.datepersonale.md
98
IV
 EXAMPLES OF CASES EXAMINED IN 2024
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
As a result, the NCPDP found a breach by the District President of the following provisions of 
the Law on personal data protection: Article 4(1)(a), (c), (e) - processing must be lawful, fair, 
adequate, relevant and limited to what is necessary; Article 6(1) - prohibits the processing of 
special categories of data, including those concerning health, except for the express exceptions 
provided by law; Article 29(1) - imposes the obligation to ensure the confidentiality of data.
Thus, the transmission to district councillors of medical records containing sensitive data was 
carried out without the valid consent of the data subjects and in the absence of another legal 
basis provided by the legislation on opersonal data protection, a fact that generated unauthorized 
disclosure of data and determined a breach of the principles of lawfulness, proportionality and 
confidentiality in the processing of personal data.
Case No. 10: Processing of personal data for the realization of a legitimate interest.
The NCPDP examined a complaint from a data subject, through which he reported the actions of 
processing his personal data by a microfinance company through the interoperability platform 
(MConnect).
Thus, during the investigation, it was found that the data subject had, at a certain period, concluded 
a loan contract with the company in question, a contract that was terminated long before the 
facts examined. Nevertheless, the company’s automated analysis system performed an automatic 
verification to update information regarding the data subject’s creditworthiness, generating access 
to personal data through the interoperability platform (MConnect), for the purpose of verifying and 
establishing the credit history and credit score, as well as transmitting them to the microfinance 
company.
These operations were carried out without human intervention, within a process of automatic pre-
approval of a personal credit limit, conducted within individual offer procedures and marketing 
activities. In this case, it was noted that the described actions were carried out in the absence of 
the data subject’s consent and after the expiration of the existing contractual relations between 
the parties.
Based on the materials presented by the controller, the absence of a legal basis for the processing 
by the company in question of his personal data, after the conclusion/closure of the contract, was 
found.
Therefore, through the described actions, the data controller breached the provisions of Article 4(1)
(a), (e) and Article 5(1) of the Law on  personal data protection.

www.datepersonale.md
99
IV
 EXAMPLES OF CASES EXAMINED IN 2024
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
Case No. 11: Processing of personal data for personal purposes 
in the exercise of the function.
The NCPDP examined a complaint from a data subject, through which he reported the actions of 
processing his personal data by an employee of a mobile telephony company.
Thus, during the investigation, it was found that the employee, whose job duties included 
providing informational support regarding the services provided by the company for clients 
choosing communication channels, having access to the employer’s information system, 
which allows viewing commercial information (type of service, assigned commercial offer, 
date, monthly invoices, payments allocated to the account, general call details, etc.) and the 
complainant’s identity data, used, during the period December 18, 2024 - January 14, 2025, 
the information system for personal purposes, accessing the complainant’s personal data 15 
times and the CDR menu (which confirms whether the phone is in Roaming or not) in the 
accounts associated with the complainant, invoking as a purpose the need to see the latter’s 
location to verify whether he was in the country or not.
In this case, the NCPDP found that the employee of the mobile telephony company processed 
personal data concerning the complainant, in the absence of a purpose and legal basis for 
processing personal data that would justify the interference in the data subject’s private life, 
actions that contravene the provisions of Article 4(1)(a), (b), (c) and Article 5(1) of the Law on 
personal data protection.

CHAPTER V
RECOMMENDATIONS AND OPINIONS 
OF THE NCPDP
In order to prevent violations of personal data protection 
regulations, as well as to ensure public awareness regarding issues 
and situations encountered, the NCPDP issues recommendations, 
which are published on the official website of the authority and 
on the institutional Facebook account and brochures/flyers are 
also prepared and disseminated during training sessions and 
public awareness activities. Similarly, to inform and raise public awareness on the importance 
of personal data protection, the video spot “Protect Your Personal Data” was broadcast, receiving 
extensive coverage through television channels.
Another instrument through which challenges, deficiencies and solutions in the field of personal 
data protection can be analyzed consists of the annual conferences organized by the NCPDP 
on 28 January, in the context of the European Data Protection Day, an event that is also widely 
publicized.
Furthermore, the NCPDP provides consultative opinions to various stakeholders regarding 
inquiries submitted on the proper application of legislation in the field of personal data 
protection.
During the reporting period, particularly in 
the context of pre-electoral and electoral 
processes, the NCPDP observed an increase 
in improper collection and use of personal 
data. These practices were reported both in 
complaints submitted by data subjects and 
through information reflected in the media, 
as well as in materials and notifications 
submitted by law enforcement authorities, 
including territorial police inspectorates, with 
cases identified involving the collection and 
disclosure of personal data, including copies 
of identity documents, for unclear or unlawful 
purposes (electoral corruption, preregistration 
on behalf of other persons for voting abroad).
Based on the multitude of situations recorded 
throughout the year, the NCPDP issued 
recommendations and warnings addressed to 
data subjects, aimed at increasing awareness, 
preventing the unjustified disclosure of 
personal data and strengthening citizens’ 
responsibility regarding the protection and 
security of personal data.

101
V
RECOMMENDATIONS AND OPINIONS OF THE NCPDP
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
In the context of the entry into force, on 23 August 2026, of Law No. 195/2024 on personal 
data protection, in 2025 the NCPDP focused on the elaboration of the document Guidelines/
Explanations for the correct application of Law No. 195/2024 on personal data protection.
The guidelines aim to facilitate the understanding and implementation of legal requirements, 
particularly given that the law marks a paradigm shift, moving from a model based on formal 
compliance to one grounded on risk assessment and management and on the proactive 
responsibility of data controllers and processors.
The document is inspired by the principles and reasoning established in Regulation (EU) 
2016/679 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 27 April 2016 on the protection of 
natural persons with regard to the processing of personal data and on the free movement of 
such data, repealing Directive 95/46/EC and aims to align national practices with international 
data protection standards, as well as to support adaptation to new technological and social 
realities.
At the same time, the NCPDP emphasized the dynamic nature of these guidelines, which can be 
adjusted and supplemented based on practical difficulties identified during the application of 
the law, recommending that the relevant parties consult them continuously.
The text of the document can be viewed by accessing the following link https://datepersonale.md/
wp-content/uploads/2026/02/CONSIDERENTE_Legea_195.pdf
Opinion of the NCPDP , following requests from a political party, expressing concerns regarding 
the legislative amendments introduced by Law No. 100/2025 for the modification of certain 
normative acts (aimed at the effective combat of electoral corruption and related aspects):
[...], the NCPDP emphasizes the necessity of treating with the utmost seriousness any processing 
of personal data that may affect the private life of data subjects, particularly when sensitive data 
are involved, such as political affiliation. The analysis/opinion in question focused specifically 
on the legal grounds for processing, its proportionality and its compliance with the fundamental 
principles of personal data protection.
First, it should be noted that the data subject to the obligation of transmission by the political 
party to the Public Services Agency and the Central Electoral Commission – the name, surname, 
personal identification number (IDNP), residence, date of joining and political party affiliation – are 
information that clearly constitute personal data and political affiliation is classified, according 
to Article 3 of Law No. 133/2011 on personal data protection, as a special category of data. 
This category of information benefits, justifiably, from an enhanced legal protection regime, as 
its unauthorized disclosure may have consequences for the dignity, freedom of expression and 
safety of the individual.
However, it should be noted that personal data protection is not an absolute right and must 
always be assessed in balance with other rights and legitimate interests of society, such as the 
transparency of public life, the integrity of the electoral process, the democratic functioning of 
institutions, etc.
Accordingly, in line with the provisions of Article 4 para. (1) letters a), b) and c) of Law No. 
133/2011 on personal data protection, the personal data subject to processing must be: processed 
correctly and in accordance with the provisions of the law; collected for specific, explicit and 
legitimate purposes and not further processed in a manner incompatible with those purposes; and 
adequate, relevant and not excessive in relation to the purpose for which they are collected and/or 
subsequently processed.

=== PAGE 102 ===
www.datepersonale.md
102
V
RECOMMENDATIONS AND OPINIONS OF THE NCPDP
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
According to Article 5 para. (5) letters b) and d) of the aforementioned law, the consent of the 
data subject is not required in cases where processing is necessary for the performance of a legal 
obligation of the controller or for the performance of tasks carried out in the public interest or in 
the exercise of public authority prerogatives vested in the controller or in a third party to whom the 
personal data are disclosed.
Analyzing the provisions of Law No. 294/2007 on political parties, it is noted that the legislator 
has explicitly established the obligation of political parties to transmit information regarding 
their members to the Public Services Agency and the Central Electoral Commission, this 
provision constituting a legal obligation, in accordance with Article 5 para. (5) letter b) of Law 
No. 133/2011.
Therefore, the transmission of personal data by political parties to the aforementioned 
authorities is legally grounded and complies with the requirements of lawfulness imposed 
by the national personal data protection legislation. Furthermore, the purposes for which this 
data are transmitted – namely: verifying the authenticity of party membership, preventing abuses 
in electoral processes and strengthening political transparency – are fully aligned with the public 
interest.
As noted in the request, the case law of the European Court of Human Rights has consistently 
reiterated that any interference with private life must have a legal basis, pursue a legitimate 
aim and be necessary in a democratic society. Cases cited, such as Rotaru v. Romania and Catt v. 
UK, condemned personal data processing lacking transparency, carried out without a clear legal 
basis or without procedural safeguards for data subjects.
In the case reported, all three conditions mentioned above are determined to be met, namely: 
1. the processing is provided for by a law adopted by Parliament; 2. the purpose is legitimate and 
relevant in the context of the functioning of a pluralistic political system; 3. procedural safeguards 
exist for the exercise of the rights of data subjects, including supervision carried out by the NCPDP.
It should also be noted that the transmission of this data does not imply generalized disclosure 
or free access to third parties, but refers exclusively to transmission to the competent public 
authorities, vested by law with clear and limited responsibilities, who are themselves obliged to 
respect the principles and requirements of personal data protection/processing.
In conclusion, under the current legal framework, the NCPDP does not identify any risks in the 
processing of personal data of party members transmitted to the Central Electoral Commission 
and the Public Services Agency, as there is an explicit legal basis established by Law No. 
294/2007 on political parties, which complies with the data protection principles provided in 
Articles 4 and 5 of Law No. 133/2011 on personal data protection.
At the same time, political parties are encouraged to adopt clear internal policies regarding 
informing members, documenting consent where necessary and implementing appropriate 
technical and organizational measures to ensure the secure transmission of the personal data 
concerned, strictly to the competent authorities.
Additionally, it should be noted that this response constitutes the opinion of the NCPDP , 
formulated under the legal provisions mentioned above, in accordance with the authority’s 
competences under the applicable legal framework for personal data protection, while the 
official interpretation of normative acts and verification of their constitutionality fall outside 
the competence of the personal data protection authority.

=== PAGE 103 ===
www.datepersonale.md
103
V
RECOMMENDATIONS AND OPINIONS OF THE NCPDP
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
The opinion of the NCPDP regarding the authority’s position on the exact retention period of credit 
history by credit history bureaus, as well as the correct mechanism for interpreting the notion 
of “credit history,” taking into account the obligation to erase information/data exceeding the 
5-year period:
From the outset, it should be noted that, with regard to the request concerning the interpretation 
and proper application of the legal provisions related to the activity of credit history bureaus, 
the NCPDP does not have the competence to provide an official interpretation of laws, as this 
prerogative belongs to the Parliament of the Republic of Moldova, pursuant to Article 66 of the 
Constitution of the Republic of Moldova.
Furthermore, in this case, it should be indicated that Law No. 133/2011 on personal data 
protection establishes the general framework of requirements and principles applicable to the 
processing of such data, regardless of the field of activity. However, to the extent that the aspects 
described have an impact on the field of personal data protection, the following considerations 
are presented:
In accordance with Article 4 para. (1) of Law No. 133/2011, personal data subject to processing 
must be processed correctly and in accordance with the provisions of the law; collected for specific, 
explicit and legitimate purposes and not further processed in a manner incompatible with those 
purposes; adequate, relevant and not excessive in relation to the purpose for which they are collected 
and/or subsequently processed; accurate and, where necessary, updated; stored in a form that allows 
the identification of personal data subjects for a period not exceeding the time necessary to achieve 
the purposes for which the data are collected and subsequently processed.
Furthermore, Article 5 of the law establishes the legal grounds on which personal data may 
be processed. Subsidiarily, paragraph (5) of the same article specifies the situations in which 
personal data may be processed without the consent of the personal data subject, for example, 
for:
●  the performance of a contract to which the personal data subject is a party, or for taking steps 
at the request of the data subject prior to entering into a contract;
●  the performance of an obligation of the controller under the law;
●  the performance of tasks carried out in the public interest or in the exercise of public authority 
prerogatives vested in the controller or in a third party to whom the personal data are 
disclosed;
●  the purposes of the legitimate interest pursued by the controller or by the third party to whom 
personal data are disclosed, provided that such interest is not overridden by the interests or 
fundamental rights and freedoms of the personal data subject, etc.
Article 11 of the aforementioned law sets out, in general terms, the conditions and retention 
periods of personal data. For example, according to para. (1), the conditions and retention periods 
of personal data are established by legislation, taking into account the provisions of Article 4 
para. (1) letter e). Upon expiration of the retention period, personal data shall be destroyed in 
the manner provided by law.
For illustrative purposes, consideration (39) of Regulation (EU) 2016/679 of the European 
Parliament and of the Council on the protection of natural persons with regard to the processing 
of personal data and on the free movement of such data and repealing Directive 95/46/
EC, is relevant to the case, which emphasizes: [...] Personal data should be adequate, relevant

=== PAGE 104 ===
www.datepersonale.md
104
V
RECOMMENDATIONS AND OPINIONS OF THE NCPDP
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
and limited to what is necessary in relation to the purposes for which they are processed. This 
requires, in particular, ensuring that the period for which personal data are stored is strictly limited 
to the minimum. Personal data should be processed only if the purpose of the processing cannot 
be reasonably fulfilled by other means. In order to ensure that personal data are not kept longer 
than necessary, the controller should establish deletion or periodic review deadlines. All reasonable 
measures should be taken to ensure that inaccurate personal data are rectified or erased. Personal 
data should be processed in a manner that ensures adequate security and confidentiality, including 
protection against unauthorized access to, or unauthorized use of, personal data and the equipment 
used for processing.
Considering that the processing of personal data may be based on various legal grounds (contract 
performance, legal obligation (Law No. 122/2008 on credit history bureaus, Law No. 308/2017 on 
the prevention and combating of money laundering and terrorism financing, regulatory acts issued 
by the competent authority), exercise or defense of a right, etc.), the retention period of personal 
data may differ depending on the intended purpose.
Under these circumstances, for the NCPDP to pronounce on the legality of the processing 
of personal data related to their storage, it is necessary to examine specific cases directly 
concerning the data subjects, based on concrete information, including in the context of a 
possible ex officio initiative.
Furthermore, analyzing the provisions of Law No. 122/2008, the NCPDP formulates an opinion 
of a consultative nature, which does not have the value of a factual finding and does not 
constitute an official interpretation of the legal provisions, but solely expresses an indicative 
point of view.
It should also be noted that the legislator, in the invoked normative act, in the provisions 
concerning credit history, uses terms in the singular and not in the plural, both when defining 
the notion and subsequently in Article 5, including Article 8 para. (1) of the law, which states that 
the credit history bureau ensures the retention of the credit history for 5 years from the date 
of the last modification of the information regarding the debtor’s obligations contained in the 
credit history [...], but not in the credit histories or in the credit history of credits. Accordingly, if, 
following a credit contract, no modifications have occurred in the credit history of that contract 
over the last 5 years, the information/data shall be destroyed/erased. The retention period 
provided by Law No. 122/2008 and the established norms apply to the credit history of each 
contract individually.
Opinion of the NCPDP on the Access to Data Concerning Administrative Fines Through the 
Interoperability Platform (MConnect), for a service intended to be launched by a commercial bank:
With reference to the request for connection to the interoperability platform, submitted within 
the context of the national commitment to digitalize public services, increase inter-institutional 
interoperability and facilitate citizens’ access to digital solutions, the commercial bank intends 
to develop an integrated digital service within a banking application for the management of 
administrative fines.
Pursuant to Article 3 of Law No. 133/2011 on personal data protection,  special categories of 
personal data  include data revealing racial or ethnic origin, political, religious, or philosophical 
beliefs, social affiliation, data concerning health status or sexual life, as well as data relating to 
criminal convictions, procedural coercive measures, or administrative fines.

=== PAGE 105 ===
www.datepersonale.md
105
V
RECOMMENDATIONS AND OPINIONS OF THE NCPDP
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
In light of the strict requirements of the provisions of Article 4 of Law no. 133/2011 personal data 
protection, personal data controllers are obligated to comply with the established principles, 
so that the personal data undergoing processing must be: processed  lawfully and fairly  and in 
accordance with the legal provisions; collected for specified, explicit and legitimate purposes and 
subsequently not processed in a manner incompatible with those purposes; adequate, relevant and 
not excessive in relation to the purposes for which they are collected and/or subsequently processed 
[…].
Similarly, Article 8 of the aforementioned law provides that the processing of personal data 
relating to criminal convictions, procedural coercive measures, or contravention sanctions 
may be carried out only by or under the control of public authorities, within the limits of their 
granted competences and under the conditions established by the laws regulating these areas.
Consequently, pursuant to Article 13 of Law no. 185/2020 regarding the automated information 
System for the recording of contraventions, contravention cases and persons who have committed 
contraventions, the participants in the System are the competent authorities empowered to 
establish contraventions, examine and resolve contravention cases.
Article 23 of the mentioned law stipulates:
(1) The right to receive information from the System is granted, within the limits of their competence, 
to:
a)  the participants in the System;
b)  criminal investigation bodies and similar bodies from other states, based on international 
treaties to which the Republic of Moldova is a party;
c)  subdivisions of public authorities and institutions in the relevant field for the purpose of 
analyzing the contravention phenomenon;
d)  natural and legal persons, with observance of the provisions of normative acts, including in 
the field of personal data protection.
(2) The request for information from the System must be justified, include the purpose and legal 
basis and contain, as the case may be, the identifiers of the case/file under examination.
(3) The persons specified in par. (1) are obliged to use the information obtained from the System only 
for the purposes provided by law.
Likewise, the provisions of the Regulation on the maintenance of the information resource 
formed by the “e-Dosar: Electronic Management of Criminal/Contravention Cases” Information 
System, approved by Government Decision no. 267/2025, are relevant, as follows:
15. The beneficiaries of data from the “e-Dosar: EMC/CC” Information System (SI) are natural persons 
and public legal persons who have access to the data within the information resource in accordance 
with the  normative framework and depending on the role assigned within the system concerning 
criminal and/or contraventional proceedings.
25. The beneficiary is obliged:
25.1. to use the functionalities of the “e-Dosar: EMC/CC” SI in accordance with the assigned roles and 
permissions;

=== PAGE 106 ===
www.datepersonale.md
106
V
RECOMMENDATIONS AND OPINIONS OF THE NCPDP
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
25.2. to use the information obtained from the “e-Dosar: EMC/CC” SI only for the purposes established 
by legislation, etc.
66. Access to data processed in the “e-Dosar: EMC/CC” SI is permitted only to authorized persons and 
entities, under the conditions of the law. Beneficiaries may include:
66.1. competent public institutions in the exercise of their legal mandates;
66.2. the data subjects, within the limits of the rights guaranteed by legislation;
66.3. contractual partners involved in service provision, with observance of data protection measures;
66.4. other judicial authorities provided by law.
Resulting from the above, data relating to contraventional sanctions constitute special categories 
of personal data, whose access and processing may be carried out exclusively by the competent 
public authorities, in strict accordance with the legal mandates and competences established 
in this regard.
Thus, in accordance with Articles 13 and 23 of Law no. 185/2020, the participants in the 
system are exclusively the competent authorities empowered to establish, examine and resolve 
contraventional cases.
Furthermore, based on the Regulation on the maintenance of the information resource formed 
by the “e-Dosar: Electronic management of criminal/contraventional cases” Information System, 
approved by Government Decision no. 267/2025, access to data within the information resource 
is permitted only to persons and entities authorized by law, depending on the assigned role and 
the legal purpose of the processing.
In this context, the possibility of extending access to such information for other categories 
of entities requires a  supplementary and rigorous analysis  from the perspective of the 
applicable  normative framework   and, possibly, corresponding legislative interventions, in 
order to ensure compliance with the principles of personal data protection and of institutional 
interoperability.

=== PAGE 107 ===
www.datepersonale.md
107
VI
ACTIVITY OF SURVEILLANCE WITH REGARD TO PERSONAL DATA PROCESSING 
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
CHAPTER VI
ACTIVITY OF PREVENTION AND 
SURVEILANCE OF PERSONAL DATA 
PROCESSING
Activity for preventing non-compliance in personal data processing
The activity of providing advice to data controllers and data subjects represents one of the 
institution’s fundamental core tasks.
Thus, during the reporting year, within the prevention segment,  1,756 consultations  were 
provided in writing, on-site, by telephone and via email.
During the reporting period, the NCPDP concentrated its activity on preventing non-compliance 
in the field of personal data processing, in the context of the legislative transition process and 
the preparation for the entry into force of Law no. 195/2024. The main objective of these actions 
was to ensure an enhanced level of anticipatory compliance and to reduce the risks generated 
by the failure to adapt internal processes to the new legal requirements.
The activities carried out primarily targeted informing and guiding data controllers and authorized 
persons regarding the legislative changes, the essential differences from the previous regulations 
and their impact on the current personal data processing activities. Furthermore, advisory support 
and methodological explanations were provided, contributing to the clarification of interpretative 
aspects and preventing the misapplication of the rules during the pre-implementation period.  
Actions of preventive analysis of existing data processing practices were undertaken, identifying 
potential non-conformities that could arise upon the entry into force of the new law. In this regard, 
general recommendations were formulated concerning the need to review internal policies, 
operational procedures and technical and organizational measures, for the purpose of gradual 
alignment with the future regulatory framework. An important role was played by the activity 
related to raising awareness among controllers and personnel involved in data processing 
regarding their responsibility, promoting the principles of a preventive approach based on risk 
assessment and the protection of data subjects’ rights from the  design phase of processes.

=== PAGE 108 ===
www.datepersonale.md
108
VI
ACTIVITY OF SURVEILLANCE WITH REGARD TO PERSONAL DATA PROCESSING 
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
These initiatives aimed to strengthen an institutional culture oriented towards compliance and 
prevention.
Under a separate heading, it should be noted that there was an increased number of requests 
and queries from citizens regarding the legal conditions for installing and using video 
surveillance systems in private spaces. The most frequently reported aspects concerned the 
clear delimitation of the filming area, with an emphasis on the need for surveillance systems 
to be directed exclusively at the owned property, without capturing images from the yards, 
dwellings, common spaces, or other areas belonging to third parties or adjacent to the public 
domain.
Furthermore, clarifications were requested regarding situations where surveillance cameras 
capture, even partially, commonly used spaces or public areas. In these cases, it was explained 
that the lack of consent from the data subjects could lead to a violation of the right to private 
life and to the submission of complaints or the imposition of sanctions. It was highlighted that 
the exclusive filming of one’s personal private space does not entail the full application of 
obligations under personal data protection legislation; however, this exception is not applicable 
when the surveillance area extends over public spaces or the properties of other persons.
In this context, attention was drawn to the fact that in situations where images from public 
spaces or others’ properties are captured, the user of the surveillance system acquires the 
status of a personal data controller, having the obligation to inform the data subjects about the 
existence of video surveillance, including by displaying visible informational signs. Through the 
information and guidance activities carried out, the aim was to prevent the violation of private 
life and reduce the risk of contraventional sanctions or the initiation of litigation, contributing 
to a responsible and proportional use of video surveillance systems by citizens.
As a result of the activities undertaken, a growing interest from controllers and citizens was 
observed for timely adaptation to the new legislative requirements and for the voluntary 
implementation of compliance measures. The prevention activity during the period preceding 
the entry into force of Law no. 195/2024 contributes to mitigating the risk of non-compliance 
and facilitating a coherent transition to the new legal regime for personal data protection.
The  NCPDP  will continue to prioritize prevention and guidance activities, including in the 
immediate period following the law’s entry into force, to ensure a uniform and correct application 
of the new legal provisions.
Under a separate heading, it is necessary to mention the  information circular/initiative  concerning 
certain obligations provided by legislation in the field of personal data protection, which was 
sent to central and local public authorities and institutions, with the following content:
The NCPDP reminds that, as of January 10, 2022, the amendments to Law no. 133/2011 on personal 
data protection entered into force, instituting the obligation for the controller and the processor to 
designate a Data Protection Officer (DPO) in the cases provided for by Article 25 of the aforementioned 
law.
In this context, we mention that, according to Article 25(1)(a) of the same law, the controller and the 
processor designate a DPO whenever the processing is carried out by a public authority or public 
institution, with the exception of courts acting in their judicial capacity.
According to Article 25(3) of the cited law, where the controller or processor is a public authority or 
public institution, a single DPO may be designated for several such authorities or institutions, taking 
into account their organizational structure and size.

=== PAGE 109 ===
www.datepersonale.md
109
VI
ACTIVITY OF SURVEILLANCE WITH REGARD TO PERSONAL DATA PROCESSING 
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
Furthermore, paragraph (4) of the same article stipulates that the DPO is designated based on 
professional qualities and, in particular, expert knowledge of data protection regulations and practices, 
as well as the ability to fulfil the tasks outlined in Article 252.
Concurrently, pursuant to Article 251(1) and (2) of Law no. 133/2011, the controller and the processor 
shall ensure that the DPO is  properly and timely involved in all issues relating to personal data 
protection and shall provide support to the DPO in fulfilling the tasks indicated in Article 25 2, by 
ensuring the necessary resources to perform these tasks, to maintain their specialized knowledge 
and to access personal data and processing operations.
Additionally, we draw attention to the fact that, according to paragraph (3), the DPO reports directly 
to the highest management level of the controller or processor.
Moreover, paragraph (6) of the same article provides that the DPO may perform other tasks and duties. 
The controller or processor must ensure that none of these tasks and duties generates a conflict of 
interest.
In the context of the above-mentioned provisions, it should be emphasized that only approximately 
50 public authorities and/or institutions have designated a Data Protection Officer.
It must be highlighted that, arising from their functional duties, the majority of public authorities 
and institutions process personal data on a significant scale. In accordance with Article 252(1) of 
Law no. 133/2011, some of the primary tasks of the DPO in relation to the controller are:
a) Informing and advising the controller or the processor, as well as employees involved in processing, 
of their obligations under this law and other normative acts;
b) Monitoring compliance with the law and other data protection-related normative acts, as well 
as with the controller’s or processor’s policies concerning personal data protection, including the 
allocation of responsibilities, awareness-raising and training activities for staff involved in processing 
operations and related audits;
c) Providing advice, upon request, regarding data protection impact assessments and monitoring 
their performance.
In this regard, the person or subdivision designated as DPO must be in a clear position to be involved 
properly and in a timely manner in all aspects related to personal data protection and must not be 
directly involved in the process of determining the purposes and means of personal data processing, 
including not being responsible for specific personal data processing operations or managed record-
keeping systems.
For advisory purposes, we refer to the Guidelines on data protection officers (WP 243 rev.01) of the 
Article 29 Working Party, according to which contradictory functions within an organization may 
include senior management positions (such as CEO, Managing Director, CFO, Head Physician, Head of 
Marketing, Head of Human Resources, or Head of IT Department), but also other lower-ranking roles 
in the organizational chart if such positions or roles lead to determining the purposes and means of 
processing.
Furthermore, it must be highlighted that the controller or processor has the obligation to publish the 
contact details of the DPO and to communicate them to the NCPDP.
Considering the  legal obligation  incumbent upon controllers/processors, in light of the 
provisions mentioned above, the NCPDP informs of the  urgent need  for public authorities/
institutions, as well as their subordinate subdivisions, to expedite the designation of a Data 
Protection Officer [...].

compliance with data exchange security and confidentiality requirements—a fact which cannot 
be fully ensured through COI technology in the process of personal data processing.
Furthermore, the e-Governance Agency has urgently requested the concerned public authorities 
to  terminate data exchange contracts  using COI technology and to undertake measures for 
the  exclusive execution  of data exchange through the  Interoperability Platform, in order 
to perform data processing/access operations via methods compatible with security and 
confidentiality requirements. Additionally, it requested the development and submission 
of concrete action plans regarding connection to the Interoperability Platform (MConnect).
Subsequently, through its initiative no. 01/3880 from 19.03.2025, the Public Services Agency 
reiterated that it has continuously undertaken the necessary measures to accelerate the process 
of terminating contracts with data-consuming institutions using COI. Thus, despite the fact that 
the involved public entities have been repeatedly notified through multiple requests regarding 
the urgency of integrating their information systems with the interoperability platform
