VII
ENDORSEMENT AND DEVELOPMENT OF DRAFT NORMATIVE ACTS
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
CHAPTER VII
ENDORCEMENT OF DRAFT 
NORMATIV ACTS
 The Procedure for Reviewing Draft Normative 
Acts constitutes an essential instrument through which 
the  NCPDP  exercises its legal mandates to ensure 
compliance with the requirements of personal data 
protection legislation in the process of developing and 
promoting public policies. Within this procedure, draft 
normative acts are examined from the perspective 
of their conformity with the principles of  legality, 
proportionality, security and transparency  in personal 
data processing, as well as respect for the rights of data 
subjects.
This chapter reflects the activity of the  NCPDP  in the 
field of reviewing draft normative acts during the reporting year and is to be supplemented 
with relevant statistical data regarding the number of opinions issued, the typology of analyzed 
projects and the main identified compliance aspects.
Throughout 2025, 208 draft national normative acts/international treaties were submitted to 
the NCPDP for review. These were examined from the perspective of protecting the rights and 
freedoms of natural persons in relation to the processing of personal data, among which were:
  39 draft international agreements/treaties;
  40 draft amendments to laws and codes;
  129 draft normative acts of the Government and other authorities.

=== PAGE 114 ===
www.datepersonale.md
114
VII
ENDORSEMENT AND DEVELOPMENT OF DRAFT NORMATIVE ACTS
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
In the case of most of the projects submitted for approval, the NCPDP highlighted the need to 
complete, adjust or revise the proposed provisions, in order to ensure their compliance with 
the national and international legal framework on personal data protection. In this regard, 
the authority formulated observations, recommendations and proposals aimed at clearly and 
coherently integrating specific provisions for personal data processing operations. 
The recommendations formulated aimed, in particular, at establishing the legal grounds for 
data processing by controllers, delimiting the categories of personal data that are the subject of 
processing, identifying the data subjects, as well as specifying the entities to which the data may 
be disclosed and the purposes for which they may be used. At the same time, aspects related to 
compliance with the principle of purpose limitation, determining storage periods, describing 
processing operations and procedures, as well as implementing appropriate technical and 
organizational measures to guarantee the security and confidentiality of personal data were 
highlighted.
Number of projects submitted for approval in the period 2009-2025
Below are some of the most relevant opinions issued on draft normative acts submitted to the 
NCPDP during 2025:
•  Regarding the review of the draft Government Decision for the approval of the concept for the 
information system for recording and managing human resources in public authorities.
In relation to the content of the draft act, specifically Chapter V concerning the organizational 
structure of SI EMRU, it was noted that, according to Article 9 of Law no. 71/2007 on registers, 
the subjects of legal relations in the field of registers include, among others, data providers and 
data beneficiaries. In this regard, the express inclusion of the SI EMRU data beneficiaries in a 
distinct section of the draft was recommended.
Concerning the interconnection of SI EMRU with other information systems, the intention to 
process salary data, including its history, as well as data on individuals’ criminal records, was 
noted. In this context, it was emphasized that data concerning criminal records and judicial 
decisions related to legal capacity constitute special categories of personal data, subject to 
an enhanced protection regime. Their processing must be strictly justified, proportional and 
appropriate to the intended purpose.

=== PAGE 115 ===
www.datepersonale.md
115
VII
ENDORSEMENT AND DEVELOPMENT OF DRAFT NORMATIVE ACTS
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
The analysis of the purposes and functions of SI EMRU, as a system designed for managing 
the organizational structure and human resources within public authorities, did not justify the 
necessity of processing these special categories of data, nor the processing of salary history 
data. Consequently, it was concluded that, pursuant to Article 4(1)(b) and Article 8 of Law no. 
133/2011 on personal data protection, the processing of such data is permitted only within 
the limits of competences expressly established by law and by authorized authorities, namely 
judicial bodies, under the conditions provided by the applicable normative framework.
Furthermore, it was mentioned that, according to the Concept of the Integrated Information 
System of Judicial Bodies, approved by Government Decision no. 1202/2006, the processing of 
data in the field of criminal justice is reserved exclusively for judicial bodies, entities which fall 
outside the competence scope of SI EMRU. Consequently, the proposed processing operations 
are incompatible with the initial purposes of data collection, in the sense of Recital (50) of 
Regulation (EU) 2016/679.
In the same context, the necessity of processing personal data regarding an individual’s ethnicity 
was not acknowledged, as it was not justified by the system’s declared purposes.
Furthermore, it was recommended to explicitly determine the  information systems  with 
which SI EMRU will ensure interoperability via the MConnect platform, as well as to specify 
the categories of personal data to be retrieved. It was clarified that the phrasing “other registers 
and information systems as data providers” is inaccurate because, according to Article 14 of Law 
no. 71/2007, the  data provider for the register  is the natural person or private/public legal 
person who submits data about the register’s subject to the registrar in the manner established 
by law or agreement and not the information system as such.
Additionally, the inclusion of clear mechanisms to ensure the information security of SI EMRU 
was proposed, particularly regarding the generation and retention of audit logs for personal 
data processing operations, as well as access for competent authorities to these logs for 
investigating potential breaches.
Finally, it was deemed necessary to supplement the draft with provisions concerning the forms 
of control exercised by the empowered authority, as well as the acts subject to liability and the 
applicable forms of legal responsibility.
•  Regarding the review of the draft Government Decision for initiating negotiations on the 
“Agreement between the Government of the Republic of Moldova and the Government of 
the Republic of India on partnership for migration and mobility” and authorizing the official 
delegation for its negotiation.
During the assessment of the draft Agreement submitted for review, it was found that it 
involves the processing of personal data. It was also highlighted that the Republic of India has 
not ratified the Council of Europe Convention for the Protection of Individuals with regard to 
Automatic Processing of Personal Data (CETS No. 108) and is not recognized as a state ensuring 
an adequate level of personal data protection, which generates increased risks regarding the 
security and confidentiality of transferred data. 
Considering the lack of clear information regarding the existence of a competent authority or a 
specific legislative framework in the field of data protection in the destination state, as well as 
the risks associated with cross-border data transfer, the need to establish additional safeguards, 
in accordance with Article 32(5) of Law no. 133/2011, was deemed necessary.

=== PAGE 116 ===
www.datepersonale.md
116
VII
ENDORSEMENT AND DEVELOPMENT OF DRAFT NORMATIVE ACTS
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
In this context, the NCPDP considered it appropriate to supplement the draft Agreement with 
distinct provisions on personal data protection, which should primarily regulate: the use of data 
exclusively for the established purposes; informing data subjects and guaranteeing their rights; 
restricting onward transfer to third parties; ensuring the accuracy, security, confidentiality and 
erasure of data; establishing retention periods; notifying incidents and unlawful processing; 
employing adequate technical security measures; maintaining records of data transfers; 
exercising control by competent authorities; and holding the parties liable for any damages 
caused.
•  Regarding the review of the draft Government Decision for the approval of the Concept and 
Regulation of the Information System for Monitoring and evaluating HIV Infection.
Regarding the content of the draft normative act, a clear description of the rights and duties 
of the data beneficiaries was recommended, as these were not provided for in either the draft 
Concept or the draft Regulation.
Furthermore, it was found that the phrase “documents pertaining to natural persons, received 
from the Public Services Agency” is formulated in a general and imprecise manner, necessitating 
the explicit determination of the types of documents and/or categories of personal data to 
be processed or retrieved within the SIME HIV. Similarly, the explicit identification of data 
categories that are of a public nature was recommended, to limit, as much as possible, the 
interference with the rights and interests of the data subjects.
Additionally, attention was drawn to the problematic nature of recording the state identification 
number (IDNP) of medical personnel in documents related to the provision of healthcare, by 
including it in observation charts, prescriptions for compensated medicines and other similar 
documents. It was reiterated that the IDNP constitutes personal data, subject to a strict regime 
of confidentiality and security and its processing must be carried out exclusively in accordance 
with legal provisions. The concurrent recording of the IDNP and the name/first name of medical 
personnel in publicly accessible documents leads to the uncontrolled circulation of this data 
and generates increased risks for the private life of the individuals concerned.
Under these circumstances, considering that SIME HIV manages the records for the informational 
object “medical personnel,” whose unique identifier is the IDNP , it was proposed, at the system 
development and implementation stage, to assign and validate a distinct identification or 
registration number separate from the IDNP , to be used in relevant medical documentation. 
The identification of medical personnel should thus be carried out via an additional numeric 
code, which would replace the IDNP on observation charts, prescriptions and other documents 
made available to patients.
The proposed mechanism, based on the random assignment of a public code different from the 
IDNP , does not affect the data controller’s right to process the medical staff’s IDNP within SIME 
HIV, exclusively for functional purposes of record-keeping and human resource management. 
This solution aims to ensure compliance in the processing of personal data by the Ministry of 
Health and to strengthen the protection of the right to private life.
In support of this approach, it was recalled that the obligation for data subjects to disclose their 
IDNP to an undetermined circle of persons has been analyzed from a constitutional standpoint 
by the Constitutional Court, which, in its decision of May 22, 2014, found the existence of an 
unjustified interference in the private life of persons engaged in liberal professions.

=== PAGE 117 ===
www.datepersonale.md
117
VII
ENDORSEMENT AND DEVELOPMENT OF DRAFT NORMATIVE ACTS
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
It was also mentioned that these proposals had been formulated by the NCPDP as early as 
March 2024, including during the review of the draft Government decision concerning the 
same information system. Nonetheless, the NCPDP was informed that the practice of recording 
the IDNP of medical personnel on certain documents related to healthcare provision persists, 
continuing to generate major risks to the inviolability of private life.
Consequently, the necessity of effectively and uniformly implementing these regulations across 
all information systems in the medical field was emphasized, in order to eliminate the use and 
recording of the IDNP of medical personnel on medical documentation provided to patients.
 •  Regarding the draft law for amending certain normative acts (Law no. 133 of July 8, 2011, on 
personal data protection; Fiscal Code of the Republic of Moldova no. 1163-XIII of April 24, 
1997).
According to the draft and its explanatory note, the legislative initiative was promoted in 
execution of  NCPDP Decision no. 300/2023  and the  Recommendation of the Parliamentary 
Commission for Human Rights no. 389/2023, which concluded that the inclusion of the IDNP on 
fiscal receipts constitutes an excessive and unjustified disclosure of personal data.
The NCPDP reiterated the existence of a deficient normative framework in the field of fiscal 
record-keeping for natural persons engaged in independent activities, as the mandatory 
inclusion of the IDNP on fiscal receipts issued by the patent holder to each client constitutes 
a disproportionate interference in private life. In this regard, the implementation of adequate 
technical and organizational measures was recommended, including the substitution or de-
identification of the IDNP or the assignment of a distinct fiscal code, to allow for fiscal record-
keeping without infringing upon the right to personal data protection.
As a compliant solution, the need for explicit regulation at the level of fiscal legislation to 
substitute the IDNP with a  unique registration code, applicable to all persons engaged in 
independent economic activities, was highlighted.
In this context, the NCPDP did not support the proposal to amend  Article 9(1) of Law no. 
133/2011 on personal data protection by introducing a new letter (c), because remedying the 
identified issue should be achieved strictly by adjusting the special normative acts in the fiscal 
domain. Law no. 133/2011 is a framework law establishing general principles applicable to all 
sectors, without regulating specific fiscal record-keeping procedures.
Furthermore, it was found that the proposed amendment is redundant and legally unclear, as it is 
already covered by the provisions of Article 9(1)(b) of the mentioned law. Moreover, conditioning 
the processing of “de-identified” data is contradictory, as de-identified data no longer constitutes 
personal data and falls outside the scope of Law no. 133/2011. The general application of this 
rule could have created difficulties in other fields where identification data is indispensable.  
In conclusion, the NCPDP assessed that the issue of inserting the IDNP on fiscal receipts can 
be resolved  exclusively by amending fiscal legislation, including by assigning a fiscal code 
distinct from the IDNP , eliminating the mandatory inclusion of the IDNP on fiscal documents 
and instituting alternative identification mechanisms.
In this sense, the NCPDP did not support the completion of Article 9(1) of Law no. 133/2011, but 
did support the amendments provided for in Article II of the draft and recommended promoting 
the necessary legislative interventions directly in the Fiscal Code and subsequent acts.

=== PAGE 118 ===
www.datepersonale.md
118
VII
ENDORSEMENT AND DEVELOPMENT OF DRAFT NORMATIVE ACTS
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
Additionally, it was emphasized that the fiscal identifier intended for persons engaged in 
independent economic activities must be  unique, automatically generated by the fiscal 
system, applicable non-discretionarily to all targeted persons and independent of the submission 
of an individual request. Such a standardized approach ensures both the efficiency of fiscal 
record-keeping and respect for the principles of legality, proportionality and data minimization.
 •  Regarding the draft law on data and data governance, developed in the context of the 
provisions of the National Programme for the Accession of the Republic of Moldova to the 
European Union 2025–2029 (Annex A, Cluster 3 – Competitiveness and inclusive growth, 
Chapter no. 10 – information Society and media).
The draft law aims to establish a normative framework for data governance, in order to ensure 
fair, transparent and secure access to data and to promote its responsible use for the benefit of 
innovation, economic development and the public interest.
It was found that the legislative initiative targets data intermediaries who may manage both 
personal data and other categories of data, which results in a direct interaction with legislation 
in the field of personal data protection. In this regard, it was emphasized that all actors involved 
in data sharing have an obligation to respect the applicable legal framework and to identify 
appropriate legal bases for the processing of personal data.
In this context, a revision of the phrasing was recommended, as it was unclear and open to 
interpretation, failing to clarify whether it referred to personal data or other data categories, 
with indications of inconsistency towards the European act to be transposed.
Concerning the mechanism of data altruism, it was noted that it is based exclusively on the 
data subject’s consent, with the possibility of its withdrawal at any time. In this context, it was 
considered appropriate to reflect in the draft the essential elements provided for by Regulation 
(EU) 2022/868, namely, the establishment of a standardized consent form for data altruism 
and the creation of a Public Register of recognized data altruism organizations, to ensure 
transparency and public trust.
Additionally, it was emphasized that the use of personal data in the public interest may involve 
large-scale processing, including of special categories of data or data concerning vulnerable 
persons, which generates high risks. In such cases, controllers must perform data protection 
impact assessments (DPIAs), with the involvement of Data Protection Officers (DPOs) and it was 
recommended, where possible, to publish their results to increase transparency.
Regarding the obligation to notify the NCPDP in case of requests for personal data, it was 
mentioned that this is insufficiently clarified, as the purpose and effects of this notification 
are not specified and the authority does not possess competencies to authorize processing 
operations. In this context, the necessity to respect the legal mandates of the data protection 
authority within future data governance mechanisms was underlined.
Finally, it was mentioned that the implementation of data governance mechanisms will 
require the substantial strengthening of institutional capacities of the competent authorities, 
particularly in terms of resources, technical expertise and operational capabilities.

=== PAGE 119 ===
www.datepersonale.md
119
VII
ENDORSEMENT AND DEVELOPMENT OF DRAFT NORMATIVE ACTS
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
•  Regarding the draft Government Decision on the organization and functioning of biobanks.
During the examination of the draft Framework Regulation on the organization and functioning 
of research biobanks, it was found that it refers, in general terms, to the existence of secure 
databases and the need to implement cybersecurity and physical security measures, without 
expressly regulating the legal framework of the biobank register.
In this context, it was emphasized that, according to Law no. 467/2003, state information 
resources must be regulated by specific documentation including the information system 
concept, functional and technical requirements, as well as the information resource regulation. 
Consequently, it was considered appropriate for the research biobank register to be regulated 
and approved as a distinct state information resource or integrated into an existing information 
system, with compliance with applicable legal, technical and security requirements. This 
approach is also supported by existing practice in similar fields, such as the information system 
for human tissue and cell banks.
Given the strategic nature of data managed by biobanks and its impact on research and public 
health, the necessity of a clear governance regime for the register was emphasized, including 
from the perspective of personal data protection.
In this regard, the applicability of the storage limitation principle under Law no. 133/2011 
was reiterated, which requires the establishment of clear retention periods for personal data 
and procedures for its destruction, archiving, transfer, or anonymization after the processing 
purpose is achieved. Consequently, the evaluation and explicit determination of these periods 
and procedures within the draft was recommended.
Furthermore, it was noted that the NCPDP does not have the competence to approve encoding 
methods used by biobanks. In this context, it was proposed to reformulate the relevant provision, 
limiting the NCPDP’s role to providing specialized consultancy on establishing an encoding 
mechanism compliant with personal data protection requirements, thus avoiding the attribution 
of competencies that exceed the legal framework.
Additionally, the obligation for data controllers to conduct a data protection impact assessment 
(DPIA) and to designate a Data Protection Officer (DPO), in situations where processing may 
generate increased risks to the rights and freedoms of data subjects, was recalled. In this sense, 
it was found that, in the case of processing regulated by the draft—which is susceptible to 
involve large-scale processing—conducting a DPIA is essential even at the stage of adopting 
the normative act.
•  Regarding the draft Government Decision on ascertaining temporary work incapacity and 
issuing the medical leave certificate.
Analyzing the content of the draft, it was found that it involves the processing of special 
categories of personal data concerning health status, including cases of illness, maternity, care 
for a sick child, quarantine, rehabilitation treatment and disability assessment.
The processing of this data is subject to Law no. 133/2011 on personal data protection and the 
applicable legal basis is Article 6(1)(b), namely, the fulfilment of legal obligations in the field 
of labour and social insurance, as well as Article 7, which permits the processing of health data 
for medical purposes and public health protection, under the supervision of personnel bound 
by professional secrecy.

=== PAGE 120 ===
www.datepersonale.md
120
VII
ENDORSEMENT AND DEVELOPMENT OF DRAFT NORMATIVE ACTS
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
In this context, the statement in the explanatory note—that the draft would have no impact 
on personal data protection—was not supported, considering this conclusion to be unfounded.
Moreover, the lack of clarity in the general provision regarding “respecting the normative 
framework in the field of data protection” was highlighted, emphasizing that Law no. 133/2011 
represents the general framework and the draft should contain specific and detailed regulations 
adapted to the particularities of the medical field and digital processing.
Another aspect that was noted is the introduction of a QR code into the digital medical leave 
certificate, without its explicit regulation in the content of the draft. The opinion warns about 
significant risks to data confidentiality and security, requesting the explicit regulation of:
•  the access mechanism via the government application;
•  the categories of data accessible through the QR code;
•  the categories of authorized persons;
•  the validity period of the QR code;
•  access logging (via Mlog) and transparency towards the holder;
•  technical and organizational security measures.
With reference to the provision stating that the insured person gives their consent for the 
automated processing and transmission of data to the employer, the opinion underlined that 
consent does not constitute an adequate legal basis in this case. The data processing occurs 
based on legal obligations and public interest and invoking consent is unjustified, especially 
under conditions of an evident imbalance between the data subject and the involved public 
authorities.
Thus, it was concluded that the use of consent in this context does not correspond to the 
principles of Law no. 133/2011, as it cannot be considered freely given, informed and without 
constraint and that the legal basis for processing must be clarified and founded exclusively on 
the applicable legal provisions, not on the formal agreement of the data subject.
Throughout 2025, the NCPDP’s activity in the legislative creation segment was focused on finalizing 
and promoting the draft of the new law on  personal data protection processed for the purpose of 
preventing and combating crime. This draft transposes Directive (EU) 2016/680 of the European 
Parliament and of the Council of 27 April 2016 on the protection of natural persons regarding 
the processing of personal data by competent authorities for the purposes of the prevention, 
investigation, detection, or prosecution of criminal offences or the execution of criminal 
penalties and on the free movement of such data and repealing Council Framework Decision 
2008/977/JHA.
The draft was elaborated and submitted to the consultation procedure. At the initiative of 
the Ministry of Justice, the draft law underwent an expertise by the European Commission, 
Directorate-General for Justice and Consumers, which expressed a positive opinion regarding the 
transposition of Directive (EU) 2016/680, appreciating the content of the draft law, considering it 
well-structured and drafted very clearly, which will constitute, together with Law no. 195/2024, 
a solid framework in the field of data protection.
In the first months of 2026, the draft of the new law on personal data protection processed for 
the purpose of preventing and combating crime is to undergo a repeated consultation procedure 
and submitted to the Parliament of the Republic of Moldova for adoption.

=== PAGE 121 ===
www.datepersonale.md
121
VII
ENDORSEMENT AND DEVELOPMENT OF DRAFT NORMATIVE ACTS
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
In relation to this aspect, it should be noted that on August 23, 2026, Law no. 195/2024 on 
personal data protection will enter into force. This legislative act establishes a new general 
normative framework in the field of personal data protection, ensuring the full transposition of 
Regulation (EU) 2016/679 into national legislation.
According to Article 2(2)(c) of Law no. 195/2024, its provisions do not apply to the processing 
of personal data by competent authorities for the purposes of the prevention, investigation, 
detection, or prosecution of criminal offences or the execution of criminal penalties, including 
safeguarding against and preventing threats to public security. This provision is identical to 
that found in Article 2(2)(d) of Regulation (EU) 2016/679 (GDPR) and is determined by the fact 
that, at the level of European Union legislation, the personal data protection processed for 
the purpose of preventing and combating crime is ensured through a distinct normative act – 
Directive (EU) 2016/680.
The establishment of a special normative framework for regulating data processing for the 
prevention and combating of crime was determined by the fact that, in the case of such 
processing, regulations must, on one hand, allow competent authorities to effectively exercise 
their mandates for preventing and combating crime and on the other hand, ensure an adequate 
level of protection for personal data. For these reasons, both Directive (EU) 2016/680 and the 
new draft law contain specific regulations addressing, in particular:
a)  The principles and legal basis for processing carried out for the purpose of preventing 
and combating crime;
b)  The manner of exercising and limiting the rights of data subjects;
c)  Ensuring the security of personal data, especially within automated information systems;
d)  Making data transfers to other states or international organizations to facilitate 
information exchange between judicial bodies.
Thus, to ensure a high level of protection for data processed for the purpose of preventing and 
combating crime and for the full harmonization of national legislation with the EU acquis in 
this field, the transposition of Directive (EU) 2016/680 through a distinct legislative act, which 
should enter into force simultaneously with Law no. 195/2024, is imperative.
In the context of the European integration process, during 2025, NCPDP employees continued 
to provide support and engagement within working groups established in the context of the 
Republic of Moldova’s accession to the European Union. Active participation was ensured in 
bilateral screening exercises, with NCPDP delegates contributing to the preparation of country 
presentations for several chapters, offering support to national authorities by providing 
necessary information for the presentations related to the following chapters:
	Chapter 2 Free Movement of Workers;
	Chapter 10 Information Society and Media;
	Chapter 23  Judiciary and Fundamental Rights;
	Chapter 24  Justice, Freedom and Security;
	Chapter 28 Consumer and Health Protection;
	Chapter 30 Common Commercial Policy.

www.datepersonale.md

VII
ENDORSEMENT AND DEVELOPMENT OF DRAFT NORMATIVE ACTS
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
Primarily, participation and reporting were ensured within the Working Group for Chapter 23 – 
“Judiciary and Fundamental Rights”—which brings together representatives from approximately 
25 public institutions—as well as within the Working Group for Chapter 24 – “Justice, Freedom 
and Security” . This involvement focused on the advancement and implementation, according 
to the established schedule, of the actions outlined in the “Rule of Law” Roadmap, approved by 
Government Decision no. 275/2025 (a reference criterion in the process of the Republic of Moldova’s 
accession to the European Union).
The “Rule of Law” Roadmap is a strategic document developed within the framework of 
negotiations for the Republic of Moldova’s accession to the European Union. This document 
establishes the strategic outcomes that the Republic of Moldova aims to achieve in the coming 
years, with the goal of consolidating the rule of law as the cornerstone of the country’s democratic 
development. The overall objective is to strengthen the independence of the judicial system, 
the protection of fundamental rights and the area of freedom, security and justice, in accordance 
with the highest EU standards pertaining to Chapters 23 and 24.
On November 4, 2025, the European Commission published the 2025 Enlargement Package, 
which is a set of strategic documents the European Commission publishes annually. These 
documents reflect the progress made by candidate countries and potential candidates wishing 
to become members of the European Union.
According to the report for the Republic of Moldova, the country has made progress on every 
negotiating chapter over the last 12 months and the overall level of preparedness for EU 
accession has increased considerably. Regarding the field of Personal Data Protection, the 
European Commission noted:
The legal framework for personal data protection has been strengthened, but the capacities of the 
data protection authority need to be improved.
The Personal Data Protection Law adopted in 2024 aims to align Moldova’s legislation with the 
GDPR. It is due to enter into force in August 2026.
In parallel, Moldova should pursue alignment with the EU Directive on data protection in law 
enforcement (Directive 2016/680), ratify the Second Additional Protocol to the Council of Europe’s 
Convention 108 and adopt the legal framework for implementing EU standards on Standard 
Contractual Clauses between controllers and processors.
The National Center for Personal Data Protection has examined a growing number of complaints, but 
its capacity should be strengthened, particularly regarding staffing.

VIIIINTERNATIONAL COOPERATION
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
CHAPTER VIII
INTERNATIONAL COOPERATION  
The year 2025 represented a period of continued consolidation and innovation for the NCPDP , 
within a dynamic and constantly evolving international landscape in the field of personal data 
protection. In a year marked by ongoing technological developments and global regulatory 
changes that continued to generate new challenges, the NCPDP maintained a consistent 
commitment to the promotion and implementation of European standards on personal data 
protection. 
In this context, the authority continued to act as an active stakeholder on the international 
stage, further strengthening existing collaborations and developing new strategic partnerships. 
Participation in international projects, working groups and forums dedicated to the protection 
of privacy proved essential for adapting to new regulatory requirements, particularly in light of 
the challenges posed by emerging technologies.
Throughout this year, special emphasis was placed on interinstitutional and multilateral 
cooperation, given the need for a global and coordinated approach to addressing threats to 
personal data security. These partnerships were essential not only for the exchange of best 
practices, but also for the identification and implementation of joint solutions capable of 
effectively responding to risks and challenges in the field.
This report reflects the achievements and steps undertaken to strengthen a global and national 
framework for the personal data protection, as well as future commitments aimed at ensuring 
a safer and more transparent digital environment for all citizens.

VIII
INTERNATIONAL COOPERATION
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
Plenary meetings of the European Data Protection Board
In 2025, the representatives of the NCPDP continued to actively participate in the activities of the 
European Data Protection Board (EDPB). During the reporting period, the EDPB held ten plenary 
meetings, six of which took place remotely and four in a physical format in Brussels. These 
plenary meetings played a key role in ensuring the consistent interpretation and application 
of Union data protection law and in strengthening cooperation among the Data Protection 
Authorities (DPAs) of the European Union.
Within the framework of the plenary meetings, the EDPB adopted a number of Opinions, 
Recommendations, Statements and Guidelines of relevance to the further development of the 
European data protection framework, including:
- Recommendations on the appropriate legal basis for requiring the creation of user 
accounts on e-commerce websites;
- Guidelines on the processing of personal data through blockchain technologies;
- Statement on the role of Data Protection Authorities (DPAs) in the context of the Artificial 
Intelligence Act (AI Act);
- Opinion on the EuroPriSe certification criteria;
- Guidelines on Article 37 of Directive (EU) 2016/680 (Law Enforcement Directive – LED) 
on the protection of natural persons with regard to the processing of personal data by 
competent authorities for the purposes of the prevention, detection, investigation or 
prosecution of criminal offences or the execution of criminal penalties and on the free 
movement of such data, repealing Council Framework Decision 2008/977/JHA, etc.
These documents were and remain of particular importance for the NCPDP , as they provide 
guidance aimed at ensuring the consistent application of Union data protection law and 
alignment with best practices across the European Union. The NCPDP’s active involvement in 
the EDPB plenary meetings contributed to strengthening the authority’s role in the development 
of European data protection policies and to ensuring a high level of personal data protection, 
including at international level.

VIIIINTERNATIONAL COOPERATION
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
Plenary meetings within the Council of Europe
The Consultative Committee of the Convention for the Protection of Individuals with regard to 
Automatic Processing of Personal Data (Convention 108)
Throughout 2025, the management of the NCPDP participated in person in the plenary meetings 
of the Consultative Committee of the Convention for the Protection of Individuals with regard 
to Automatic Processing of Personal Data (Convention 108).
During these meetings, a variety of significant topics were discussed, including Convention 
108+ and the current status of ratifications and accessions, personal data protection in the 
context of combating money laundering and terrorist financing, contractual clauses in cross-
border data flows, the interpretation of Article 11 of modernised Convention 108 in relation to 
privacy-enhancing technologies, cooperation with other bodies and entities of the Council of 
Europe, as well as major developments and activities in the field of data protection.
The plenary meetings demonstrated the dynamic and evolving nature of the European legal 
framework for personal data protection. The Consultative Committee of Convention 108 continues 
to play a central role in guiding public policies, shaping ethical principles for digitalisation and 
promoting responsible data governance at the global level.
Participation in these sessions provided a valuable opportunity for the exchange of expertise 
and the strengthening of international cooperation, reaffirming the commitment of the Republic 
of Moldova to the protection of fundamental rights in the digital era. During the meetings, 
the NCPDP delegation presented the status of the ratification of Protocol CETS No. 223 to 
Convention 108, noting that on 25 July 2024, the Parliament of the Republic of Moldova adopted 
Law No. 195/2024 on personal data protection, which was published in the Official Gazette on 
23 August 2024. The law fully transposes the GDPR and is scheduled to enter into force within 
two years from its publication in the Official Gazette of the Republic of Moldova.
The draft law on the ratification of the Protocol amending the Convention for the Protection of 
Individuals with regard to automatic processing of personal data, together with the accompanying 
documentation, was submitted to the Ministry of Justice for the intergovernmental consultation 
procedures. In this context, the Ministry of Justice considered it appropriate for the Protocol to 
be ratified concurrently with the entry into force of Law No. 195/2024, on 23 August 2026.

CHAPTER IX
AWARENESS AND TRAINING 
ACTIVITIES
During the year 2025, the NCPDP continued to make significant progress in fulfilling its 
objectives, focusing in particular on the development and implementation of activities aimed 
at informing and raising awareness among the general public about the importance of the field 
of personal data protection. In this context, a series of educational initiatives and awareness-
raising campaigns were carried out, which, on the one hand, tried to explain the rights of 
individuals regarding the handling of their personal data and, on the other hand, to promote 
responsible behavior with respect to their protection. 
Through these actions, the NCPDP pursued not only an increase in understanding of the legal 
framework in the field but also the promotion of an organizational and social culture in which 
respect for the confidentiality and security of personal data becomes an integrated practice 
in the daily life of every data subject. This approach aims not only to ensure compliance with 
national and European legislation, but also to strengthen public confidence in the process of 
personal data protection.
The activities carried out were addressed both to the general public and to representatives of 
public authorities and school communities through the following actions: 
	Training sessions organized in physical, online and hybrid formats, adapted to the needs 
of the participating institutions; 
	Information campaigns and street awareness actions designed to enhance the general 
public’s awareness of personal data protection; 
	Participation in relevant national events to promote the right to privacy and best practices 
in the field; 
	Inter-institutional collaboration with the General Police Inspectorate (GPI), General 
Inspectorate of Border Police (GIBP), General Inspectorate for Migration (GIM), Customs 
Service (CS), as well as other public institutions and private sector entities.

Street Information and Awareness-Raising Activities
A series of street information and awareness-raising activities targeting the general public in the 
field of personal data protection were organized within distinct thematic contexts, contributing 
significantly to increasing societal awareness.
Thus, on 29 January 2025, in the context of the celebration of the European Data Protection 
Day  and on 07 July 2025, on the occasion of the 17th anniversary of the NCPDP, representatives 
of the institution organized street actions in the area of the Triumphal Arch in Chișinău. During 
these activities, a group of NCPDP staff distributed informational materials to passersby, aiming 
to raise awareness and promote best practices in privacy and personal data protection. Citizens 
were informed about the concept of personal data, the rights of data subjects, the principles 
of personal data protection, as well as the security and confidentiality measures that must be 
respected during data processing. At the same time, examples of non-compliant processing of 
personal data were presented and practical guidance and recommendations were provided 
regarding the actions that can be taken in such cases.
Through the implementation of these activities, the NCPDP reaffirms the importance of personal 
data protection as a matter of major public interest, emphasizing that ensuring respect for this 
fundamental right constitutes a shared responsibility, requiring a constant commitment from 
public authorities, organizations and every individual citizen.

Information and Awareness Campaign “Maximum Vigilance in Sharing/
Providing Personal Data””
During the year 2025, the NCPDP , in collaboration with the General Police Inspectorate and 
the General Inspectorate of Border Police conducted an information and awareness campaign 
entitled “Maximum Vigilance in Sharing/Providing Personal Data” , aiming to raise citizens’ 
awareness of the risks associated with the uncontrolled disclosure of personal data.
The activities were carried out in public spaces with high foot traffic, including in traffic, at 
Chișinău International Airport, as well as within the territorial subdivisions of the GPI, regional 
directorates of the GIBP and border crossing points, with the involvement of specialized data 
protection structures within the partner institutions. During the campaign, citizens were 
provided with informational materials and practical recommendations on protecting personal 
data contained in identity documents, bank cards and other similar documents. They were 
warned about the risks arising from sharing copies of such documents or providing personal 
data for inclusion in various lists or forms without verifying the existence of a legal basis. 
Additionally, the necessity of checking the legality and purpose of any request for data from 
third parties was emphasized.

CHAPTER X
MANAGERIAL ACTIVITY OF NCPDP
Institutional performance of the NCPDP is directly influenced 
by the professional competence of its staff, their level of 
specialization and the results achieved in performing their 
functional responsibilities. At the same time, an analysis of 
employee efficiency highlights the importance of institutional 
management in ensuring the respect of staff rights, implementing 
effective leadership practices and maintaining a safe and 
conducive working environment.
According to the structure, approved by Law no. 182/2008, the 
NCPDP consists of 8 structural subdivisions and the number of staff is 45 units, including 
auxiliar staff. These indicators establish the organizational and regulatory framework within 
which the NCPDP operates.
At the end of 2025, the total number of employees was 36 employees, representing a shortfall 
of 9 positions compared to the staffing - limit. Of the total employed personnel, 2 held the 
status of public dignitary, 32 were civil servants (including 10 managerial-level civil servants 
and 22 operational-level civil servants), 1 held a technical service position and 1 served as 
auxiliary staff.
Under these circumstances, the occupancy rate of public positions and posts provided for in the 
staffing table was approximately 80%, reflecting an institutional capacity below the maximum 
approved level. This situation necessitated workload redistribution and the assumption of 
additional responsibilities by the employed staff, directly impacting the employee workload, 
the pace of institutional objective achievement and the complexity of activities performed.

Professional training
In order to strengthen institutional capacity, the NCPDP prioritizes human resource development, 
considering it a key factor in enhancing the efficiency of its activities and improving the quality 
of services provided.
The continuous professional development of staff is carried out in accordance with the provisions 
of Annex No. 10 to Government Decision No. 201/2009, which regulates the application of Law 
No. 158/2008 on the Public Service and the Status of Civil Servants, as well as based on the 
annual continuous professional training plan of the Data Protection Authority.
During the reporting period, 36 employees participated in various training activities, including 
individuals whose employment contracts were later terminated. These activities took place in 
different forms and formats, as follows:
•	 8 external training sessions organized by the National Institute of Public Administration 
and Management, the reference institution in promoting state policies in the field of professional 
training and development of civil servants at all levels;
•	 10 internal training sessions, including trainings on specific activity areas, conducted by 
the institution’s internal trainers.
At the same time, NCPDP staff participated in official business trips abroad, aimed at exchanging 
experience with similar institutions and adopting their best practices. These activities were 
carried out through study visits, workshops, conferences, as well as participation in various 
high-level events and meetings.
Economic and financial activity
The economic and financial management policy of the NCPDP 
is aimed at promoting accountability and integrity in the use 
of public resources, as well as at continuously improving the 
authority’s budget management mechanisms.
In accordance with Article 19 of Law No. 133/2011 on personal 
data protection, the NCPDP is fully financed from the State 
Budget, within the limits of the budgetary allocations approved 
by the annual budget law.
Pursuant to State Budget Law No. 310/2024, for the year 2025, 
the NCPDP was allocated MDL 12,605.6 thousand. During the year, the institution’s budget 
was adjusted, with an additional MDL 50.0 thousand allocated under Government Decision No. 
639/2025 to mitigate budgetary risks related to energy services.

PROBLEMS AND OBJECTIVES IN THE ACTIVITY OF THE NCPDP
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
The difficulties faced by the NCPDP over the years largely persist during the period under review, 
with the institution continuing to face legislative, institutional, functional and public-perception 
challenges, which directly affect the efficiency of the exercise of its statutory responsibilities, its 
institutional responsiveness and the sustainable development of the personal data protection 
field at national level.
Although positive developments have been recorded, including the modernization of the 
general regulatory framework through the adoption of Law No. 195/2024, which will enter into 
force on 23 August 2026, the structural problems identified in previous periods persist, having 
a systemic nature and requiring coordinated interventions at both legislative and institutional 
levels. The main challenges identified are as follows:
- The need to adopt the draft law on personal data protection processed for the purposes of 
preventing and combating crime. The adoption of Law No. 195/2024 on the personal data 
protection represents a significant step forward in the process of harmonising national 
legislation with the European Union acquis, ensuring the faithful transposition of Regulation 
(EU) 2016/679 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 27 April 2016 on the protection of 
natural persons with regard to the processing of personal data and on the free movement of such 
data and repealing Directive 95/46/EC.
However, the regulatory framework remains incomplete in the absence of a distinct and 
comprehensive regulation governing the processing of personal data by competent authorities 
for the purposes of the prevention, detection, investigation and prosecution of criminal offences, 
as well as the execution of criminal penalties.
With the repeal of Law No. 133/2011 on personal data protection, the lack of a special law 
transposing Directive (EU) 2016/680 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 27 April 
2016 on the protection of natural persons with regard to the processing of personal data by 
competent authorities for the purposes of the prevention, detection, investigation, or prosecution 
of criminal offences or the execution of criminal penalties and on the free movement of such 
data and repealing Council Framework Decision 2008/977/JHA, will create a regulatory vacuum 
concerning the legal regime applicable to such processing operations. This situation will 
adversely affect both the protection of the fundamental rights of data subjects and the legal 
certainty of the authorities involved.
The relevant draft law has been prepared and submitted for inter-institutional review, as well 
as for expert assessment by the European Commission, General – Department for Justice and 
Consumers; however, it is imperative to accelerate its promotion and adoption in the near term, 
so that the legislative act enters into force concurrently with Law No. 195/2024.
- The impact of the number of petitions addressed to the NCPDP concerning civil-law disputes. 
During the reporting period, the authority was notified of a substantial number of petitions 
submitted by citizens, a significant proportion of which relate to civil-law disputes (such as 
PROBLEMES AND OBJECTIVES 
IN THE ACTIVITY OF THE NCPDP

=== PAGE 146 ===
www.datepersonale.md
146
PROBLEMS AND OBJECTIVES IN THE ACTIVITY OF THE NCPDP
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
disputes between neighbours or spouses, contractual disagreements, or cases pending before 
courts or administrative bodies). Although such matters should be resolved through legal 
mechanisms specific to civil law, petitioners frequently invoke, in a formal manner, alleged 
infringements of personal data protection legislation.
The analysis of these petitions indicates that, in many cases, data protection issues do not 
constitute the core element of the complaint, leading to the allocation of resources to the 
examination of matters that fall outside the authority’s mandate. This situation limits the 
institution’s capacity to focus on relevant cases requiring in-depth analysis and complex 
involvement.
In this context, strengthening the preliminary complaint assessment procedure, in line with the 
authority’s statutory responsibilities, remains a priority, in order to ensure the optimal use of 
institutional resources and to focus activities on core areas of competence
- The discriminatory level of remuneration of NCPDP civil servants. The current level of 
remuneration of NCPDP staff continues to be lower than that applicable to other public 
authorities with comparable control and supervisory mandates, including institutions that 
process significant volumes of personal data and are subject to oversight by the NCPDP . This 
salary disparity directly affects the institution’s ability to attract and retain qualified specialists 
in a field characterised by a high degree of technical and legal complexity and by a pronounced 
national shortage of experts.
The remuneration mechanism currently applicable to NCPDP staff does not adequately reflect 
the responsibility, complexity and risks associated with the activities performed and, in effect, 
runs counter to the principles of equality, equity and coherence enshrined in Law No. 270/2018 
on the unified public sector remuneration system. The persistence of this situation seriously 
undermines the institutional capacity of the NCPDP and jeopardises its role in the context of 
the Republic of Moldova’s European integration process
- The insufficient number of staff in relation to the specific nature and the steadily increasing 
workload. The insufficient number of employees, particularly within the General Department 
of Surveillance and Conformity and the Legal Department, is disproportionate to the growing 
volume and diversity of activities carried out by the NCPDP . The same civil servants are 
simultaneously involved in the examination of petitions, the conduct of compliance inspections, 
the exercise of duties as ascertaining agents, the representation of the institution before courts 
in administrative and contraventional proceedings, the drafting and endorsement of draft 
normative acts, as well as in training and professional development activities and participation 
in bilateral screening exercises within the European integration process.
This constant overwork, in the absence of effective institutional mechanisms for workload 
redistribution and internal capacity-building, adversely affects the quality of administrative 
decision-making, procedural timelines and staff stability. The situation is further exacerbated 
by the low level of remuneration, which leads to staff turnover and the loss of accumulated 
institutional memory and expertise.
- The lack of adequate safeguards for NCPDP staff. NCPDP personnel do not benefit from 
appropriate institutional safeguards commensurate with the risks inherent in supervisory and 
control activities, particularly in cases involving sensitive matters or the oversight of entities 
with a high degree of influence. Instances have been identified of pressure, intimidation, or 
abusive challenges to the actions of staff members carried out in the performance of their

=== PAGE 147 ===
www.datepersonale.md
147
PROBLEMS AND OBJECTIVES IN THE ACTIVITY OF THE NCPDP
ACTIVITY REPORT FOR THE YEAR 2025
official duties, before various bodies, by petitioners or entities subject to inspection.
This vulnerability undermines the functional independence of the supervisory authority and 
creates conditions that may inhibit the impartial and effective exercise of statutory powers by 
employees, making it necessary to establish additional safeguards to protect staff involved in 
control and sanctioning activities
- The inefficiency and insufficiency of coercive enforcement mechanisms in cases of unlawful 
processing of personal data. Until the full entry into force of the new law on personal data 
protection, the coercive mechanisms available to the NCPDP have been affected by the dual 
and contradictory nature of the procedures for challenging the acts issued by the authority. 
This situation has manifested itself in the parallel examination of the same findings and 
administrative acts both in administrative litigation proceedings and within contraventional 
procedures.
The duplication of procedures has reduced the dissuasive effect of the sanctions imposed 
and has undermined the effectiveness of supervisory activities. It is anticipated that the new 
provisions of Law No. 195/2024 will contribute to clarifying and enhancing the efficiency of 
procedural and sanctioning mechanisms.
- The abusive invocation of personal data protection provisions. There continues to be a practice 
among certain information providers of unjustifiably invoking personal data protection rules to 
refuse the provision of information of public interest. This erroneous approach distorts the 
purpose of the legislation in the field and undermines the exercise of the constitutional right of 
access to information, as well as the transparency of public authorities’ activities.
- Continued access to personal data stored in state automated information resources via the 
SIC “ Acces-Web” and COI systems. In many cases, the identification of the user, the purpose and 
the legal basis for access is limited or hindered, creating significant difficulties in ensuring and 
monitoring the traceability of data access.
In this context, there is a clear need to extend access to state registries and information systems 
through the interoperability platform (MConnect) as a mechanism to enhance accountability 
and control over data processing
- The low level of public awareness regarding the risks associated with the disclosure of 
personal data. Despite information campaigns and awareness-raising activities, the population’s 
understanding of the risks linked to the uncontrolled disclosure of personal data remains 
insufficient. Individuals continue, under various pretexts, to provide copies of identity documents 
and sensitive data (such as IDNP , bank details, income, pensions, etc.) without verifying the 
legality of the collection or the purpose for which these data will be used.
This behaviour increases the risk of fraud, misuse of data and violations of the right to privacy, 
highlighting the need to intensify public education and awareness-raising activities
The objectives of the NCPDP for 2026 are essential to take appropriate action to address the 
concerns highlighted above. Thus, the basic objectives outlined for the immediate future, but 
not limited to those described below, will focus on ensuring:
	Implementation of the necessary actions to ensure the proper functioning of the NCPDP 
upon the entry into force of Law No. 195/2024 on personal data protection, both in terms 
of institutional management and through the drafting and adjustment of the internal 
regulatory framework.

=== PAGE 148 ===
www.datepersonale.md
148
PROBLEMS AND OBJECTIVES IN THE ACTIVITY OF THE NCPDP
NATIONAL CENTER FOR PERSONAL DATA PROTECTION OF THE REPUBLIC OF MOLDOVA
	Alignment of the national legal framework on personal data protection with the latest 
European regulations, through the approval by the Parliament of the Republic of Moldova 
of the draft law on personal data protection for the purpose of preventing and combating 
crime.
	Strengthening the administrative and institutional capacities of the NCPDP, particularly 
regarding human resources, including through activities aimed at improving the skills 
and knowledge of employees.
	Continuing to carry out tasks derived from the National Action Plan for the Republic of 
Moldova’s accession to the European Union for 2024–2027 and from the Roadmap approved 
by Government Decision No. 275/2025 for the approval of the “Rule of Law” Roadmap (a 
reference criteria in Moldova’s EU accession process).
	Continuing and expanding public awareness activities on the importance of personal data 
protection, both from the perspective of respecting/understanding data subjects’ rights 
and ensuring the fulfilment of obligations by personal data controllers.
	Contributing to the correct interpretation and proper application of legal provisions in 
the field of personal data protection by actors involved in data processing, including by 
ensuring a balance between legal provisions related to the right of access to public 
information, freedom of expression and personal data protection.
	Raising awareness among development partners in the implementation of joint projects to 
ensure an adequate level of personal data protection in the Republic of Moldova.
